ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e227	We're dealing with today Report. St. 16 for 2010–2011, National Health and Care Plan 2011–2015. As rapporteur, I would like to thank the Committee for its good and constructive cooperation. We have had three major cases at the same time, which have required a lot of work. But we've been working together on the good results that are now available. Many are in agreement, but in some places the political divides are made clear. There is a broad consensus on the major outlines. It is important when we make plans and laws with such a great impact on the health care of the future. What we are doing now is one of the greatest changes in health in our time, and much consensus ensures predictable and secure frameworks. The Committee has had a large and useful consultation round. Thank you to everyone who recorded to the committee - several of them can be found in the setting. National health and care plan shows the long-term path of the Interaction Reform, which was adopted last year. Here the main objectives are elaborated: proper treatment in the right place at the right time, and how to interact better. Last year we made the diagnosis, now comes the medicine. This is the political course that takes the legal, organisational, economic and professional steps needed to bring about the reform. Norwegian public health is good, and we have one of the world's best health services. However, sadly, someone feels that they are not getting the help they need when they need it or that the treatment is being fragmented. Our goal is to change and improve this. Prevention and public health permeate future health thinking. We need to prevent more in order to repair less. What we are preventing today will not have the greatest effect tomorrow. We have to think about the future to see the results. That's why school and kindergarten are some of the most important arenas to start at. We have to have patience, because change takes time. Municipalities are the main prevention arena, and they are now required to identify their own health challenges and initiate action. In this way, they affect the health of the population. They must also cooperate with the voluntary and non-profit sector, for health should be thought of at all stages and levels, and by all. The interaction reform gives municipalities more responsibility for their own citizens, and it must be economically interesting to initiate measures. Therefore, co-financing is introduced. Municipal co-financing is the link between the specialist health service and the municipality. The Committee disagrees on how this should be done. The majority believe that co-financing gives municipalities something to go after and is a positive intuition, while the minority wants another device, which I expect them to explain. Co-financing means that municipalities have to pay part of the bill on deposits. It will help to build up alternative services in municipalities, with a good care and care service that can do more than it does today. In co-financing, there are some important exceptions in the case of insertions, such as operations, births, and there is a ceiling of NOK 30000 for specific single-beds. In addition, there is no provision for municipal co-financing for mental health protection, drug treatment and housing in private training institutions. Although mental health and alcohol are now excluded, this does not mean that responsibility is not placed. Again, municipalities must think completely and focus on the whole life cycle, on housing and prevention. Municipalities and hospitals are required to conclude cooperation agreements to facilitate treatment close to the patient’s place of residence. Many municipalities have already established local medical centres where specialist health services and contract specialists are important partners. Such centres may contain different functions and contribute to faster treatment of good quality. This means that those who often need healthcare can receive treatment in their community. Municipalities shall be responsible for those with a clear picture of the disease, while where it is unclear or is a serious disease, the hospital is the place. So this change doesn't mean that patients don't go to hospitals anymore, but it'll be like today, that it's the disease image and the doctor together with the patient who decides where you're going to be. Municipalities need to build up offers for ready-to-print patients, with responsibility from the first day. They are also required to provide immediate help and daily services for patients in need of treatment or observation. This will be phased in until 2015. Municipalities can prevent the need for hospitalisation through accident prevention and drug prevention. They can reduce mismedication and they can add community pharmacists. A lot is already happening in municipalities today that can be further developed, and the majority believe it should continue. The municipalities are on their way. The minority wants pilots and things to happen a little bit slower. The decision maker has a major and important role to play. He is a central part of the local health service and is the link between first and second line services. This competence should be better exploited by enabling much of what is done at the specialist level to be done by the permanent medical practitioner. Municipalities can develop outpatient, multi-disciplinary teams linked to the local medical centres, and they can complement them with the follow-up of chronicler groups and their families. A Joint Committee considers that there is a need for a stronger focus on rehabilitation and rehabilitation, and that the gap between specialist rehabilitation and municipal rehabilitation needs to be reviewed. In particular, older people and chroniclers want to benefit from community rehabilitation. Regardless of how this is established, it shall be a multidisciplinary, good-quality scheme. The specialist healthcare service needs to be further developed and new methods and instruments applied. It is important that new things that should be centralised are centralised, while what can be done equally well at the local level should be done there. In this context, the role of local hospitals is important, and there must be broad competence and good quality there. The Committee disagrees with what a local hospital should contain and how detailed it should be described, but the notification indicates some functions that should be present – such as X-rays, laboratories and specialists with broad competence. We in the Red-Greens believe that there should be anaesthesia in those hospitals with emergency preparedness that already have anaesthesia today, and believe this complements what the government itself writes about the content. The Advance Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party want a minimum standard for local hospitals. As important as good hospitals are good prehospital services for a quick and safe treatment to the right place. As far as ICT is concerned, the Committee is in agreement and believes that national governance in the field of ICT development should be provided for in the future. Everything that's exciting has to be further developed. I mention eRecept, more use of electronic messaging systems, national health portal and national core journal. Welfare technology must also be further developed. Patient safety, ethics, research, management, education and quality are factors that are important in the health care of the future and which both the notification and the suspension highlight well. We are concerned about the future shortage of health professionals. We need to ensure that personnel are recruited, trained and maintained. The quality promise 2015 contributes, but more needs to be done, and more young people need to choose health as their profession in the future. The communication aims at preventing and eradicating social health inequalities. At the same time, it provides the direction of health policy: more responsibility for municipalities and more clear clarification of what hospitals should do. For the first time, a municipal politician will know what specialist health services do. The reform is a declaration of trust to the municipalities, which we believe can do the job. The municipalities are more than ready. They're already running. There are an incredible number of great, exciting measures that are ready, and that they are using now, but we know that they can do even more. Prevention takes time. The benefits of the work we are now presenting will be seen in the long term. That we're on the right track is indisputable. This message and the law are for the benefit of patients, which has been our goal all along. I look forward to the debate here today on the health care of the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e319	As the rapporteur for the Health and Care Act, I would first like to thank the Committee for its clean and proper work, which has made it possible to deal with the matter here today. That's my thanks for giving on behalf of the whole committee. At the same time, I want to wish him good recovery during his medical report, also on behalf of the entire committee. The most important starting point for the new law is, of course, the cooperation reform. However, the Committee also stresses that the many legislative amendments have not been triggered by the cooperation reform alone, but that there has been a long need to coordinate and clean up municipal laws and laws in some neighbouring areas. And it is important to stress that despite the fact that the government parties rejected the wide-ranging resolution of the white opposition, all parties in this room share the same starting point, namely a desire for more focus on prevention and rehabilitation. We also share the basic view that we want to treat more people as close to where they live as possible. One concern the opposition has is that one with what appears to us to be urgent has damaged the consultation period, and that, with amendments to more than 41 laws, including: changes in the Patient Rights Act and the Special Health Service Act – and in addition an extensive use of powers – are almost irresponsible to deal with this in such a short time, not least when it is also expected that Norwegian municipalities will be ready to implement it as early as next year. This is essentially a difficult problem. A single committee stresses that the overall objective of the legislative proposal is to facilitate better interaction, greater multidisciplinary cooperation and comprehensive solutions in municipalities. The population shall be guaranteed equal access to good and equivalent health and care services, irrespective of diagnosis or need, age, sex, personal economy and residence. The Committee also considers that the current law and regulation currently in place of cooperation between administrative levels are not sufficient to meet patients’ needs for comprehensive and coordinated health and care services. Reference is made to the fact that when municipalities and enterprises are to cooperate and plan treatment processes, this should be based on the LEON principle and the statutory distribution of responsibilities. The entire Committee welcomes the use of the contractual institution to promote interaction and considers it reasonable to impose obligations on municipalities and undertakings to conclude cooperation agreements. The Committee also considers it important that the national framework agreement, standard agreement and guidance material strive to achieve a high level of equality in the form of the agreements to be concluded and the Committee agrees to the establishment of an advisory dispute resolution panel based on the model of the Child Protection Dispute Resolution Board to deal with disagreement between the parties. The Committee also considers it natural that any disagreement may be brought before the courts if the parties do not wish to follow the recommendation of the dispute settlement officer. Cooperation agreement between most municipalities and their nearest hospital is currently being concluded. on ready-to-print patients, and there is wide professional contact between general practitioners and hospitals about the patients for whom they share joint responsibility. A large number of municipalities have gradually entered into cooperation with hospitals to provide decentralised specialist health services, including: in the much-mentioned local medical centre. This established cooperation will provide a good basis for more extensive and formalised cooperation. In addition, I am pleased that the entire Committee warns that one of the consequences of the elimination of the distinction between health services and care services can be reversed so that services for which no self-payment can be required today can now be subject to self-payment, as well as a unanimous committee asking the Ministry to follow these developments closely. The undersigned is also pleased that the Committee sees that the financial instrument can lead to additional burdens for periods such as: for holiday municipalities and student municipalities, etc. With regard to the main approach to funding itself, it has not been possible to reach an agreement. At this point, it is natural to go over to present the specific comments and proposals of the Progress Party. The fact that we're dealing with more cases today, makes me also going to get into some of these. The issues dealt with by the Committee illustrate the political difference between the Red-Green Government and the policy of the Progressive Party and the policy on which the bourgeois parties are largely united. We are distinguished from the red-greens in terms of patient safety, professional neutrality and the funding model – both of the extraction from hospitals and not least the so-called co-financing. The fact that the specialist health service is to be reduced by 5 billion. kr, there is disagreement about. The bourgeois parties have the basic starting point that matters, and this reform, should be built from below and up, not above and below. And not least, the bourgeois parties have an understanding – which we do not register with the red-green – that it will take time to build up the competences required by the municipalities in this matter. When we all know, as we know, among other things. Discussed last Friday, that the rug is a situation in regional health companies with major economic and, not least, professional challenges, I believe that we are entering a dangerous land, and that, as I also said on Friday, with a government and a government council that does not have full control over regional health companies. Last week's events reinforce the need for what the Progress Party has said many times, as well as pointed out in the handling of the matters that we are going to vote on today. In my view, the events show that both the time of regional health companies is over and that this unnecessary and very bureaucratic link should be removed. In my view, they also show that funding needs to be changed. In fact, it is a paradox, at least for the undersigned and for the Progress Party, that the Minister of Health will allow the economic bottling of health businesses to continue through large and irresponsible cutbacks, while at the same time we have 280,000 people in line. And now it has to be cut further and transferred to the municipalities on the matter we are dealing with today. The solution of the Advance Party – we want the hospital services to be funded by the state, and the money to follow the patient – is well-known, and it cannot be repeated too often. The party clearly says no – and so does the civil parties – to a so-called municipal co-financing for hospital treatment. This will increase the threshold for hospitalisation, primarily for older people, and it will increase the geographical health differences that we have in this country. And it will in the long run drain an already heavily pressed specialist health service for even more money and perhaps also for competence. So instead of dragging the government's proposals over the heads of the Municipality and Health-Norway through a top-down position, the Progress Party wants the interaction arenas to be created from below and up, through good local pilot projects around the country. We want such local initiatives to provide the basis for interaction, so that we can learn from the good projects out there and pass on the good experience to others across the whole country. I was going to go into a lot of other things too, but time only now allows me to promote the proposals on which the Progress Party is involved. This concerns all the matters we have to deal with that we are now dealing with together, and I consider the proposals to be referred to.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e395	The background that today’s Storing will focus on prevention and early intervention in health policy is based on three key analyses of future challenges. One is that we know that 70 pst. of all serious diseases treated in hospitals are life-style related. We can't solve it in the future just by increasing treatment capacity, we have to do something more. We must give priority to early action; we must give priority to prevention. Number two: As a result of our high standard of living, we live longer, and the need for healthcare and health professionals will rise dramatically over the next 15-20 years. If we don't take action now, there will be a need for all the young people leaving high school, in health care, and that won't work. I would like to draw up one more factor: we have more health differences in Norway than they have in countries where it is natural to compare ourselves with, and we are spending less on prevention health. We can't wait for the challenge to get acute, we have to start now. It's the long-term perspective that's being taken into account today. I have been the case rapporteur for the Public Health Act and will begin by commenting on it. I would first like to thank the Committee on Good Cooperation. There is agreement on most of it. The Public Health Act is a milestone. It's a milestone that we're now going to adopt a law that aims to combat social health inequalities. It will require considerable efforts across all sectors, with long-term target awareness. The Public Health Act stresses that public health is not just an individual responsibility but a responsibility for the community. One of the most important things we do with the Public Health Act is to shift the responsibility for health. It is too subtle when only healthcare is to be responsible for health. 90 pst. public health activities are to be carried out outside the health sector. With this law, public health responsibility is transferred to an overall municipal level. One of the main principles of the law is that there should be health in everything we do. A 2010 public health report on the state of health in Norway confirms with almost brutal honesty how severe social differences work. One does a theoretical exercise where one sees what would have been the effect of raising the population to the health level of the best-placed groups. Then it comes in response that every year 1000 dies for many of the heart diseases and 500 for many of the lung cancers. A total of 4,300 lives could have been saved annually. It gives us a clue about the importance of combating health inequalities in society. It is therefore the second principle of the law that it should work in a balanced way; it should combat social health differences. Volunteering is very important for public health. Sports teams, cultural organisations and children's and youth organisations contribute to the arenas for belonging, mastering and rejoicing for a great many of us. That is also why the majority of the Committee proposes to strengthen the legal text relating to the responsibilities of municipalities for volunteering. With the new law, municipalities should facilitate voluntary activities. The setting-up of this is, of course, a question that needs to be addressed locally and in close cooperation with the volunteer itself. This work is part of the third principle, the principle of participation. Participation is equally fully concerned with the need for each individual to take care of his or her own health, while at the same time enabling the authorities to make good choices. One of our strongest maps to ensure good health for the population is a well-regulated working life. When the NHO organized its annual conference in January under the Velferdsfallen vignette, they set out to show that the population is working less. In 1850, says the NHO, about half of our waking time was spent at work, in 1950 about 35 p.m. and in 2000 about 25 pst. But this is progress. It's a tractor rather than a horse. We probably worked more in 1850, but what else was society like? In 1886, life was 51 years old, in 1950 it was increased by 20 years, in 2000 it was almost 76 years old, and this year it is even higher. It is about increasing living standards and medical progress. Having a working life that people can live with is essential for health and well-being, for social development and for how long we get to live. It manifests clearly: the pastor can expect 81 years of life, the waiter and the taxi driver ten fewer years. The Public Health Act is a major effort – a strengthened labour-environment law, a strong public health service with low self-esteem, environmental and public transport efforts to improve local air conditions. The Public Health Act provides municipalities with strong instruments to ensure that the health aspect is included in all local planning and social development. Many municipalities and county councils have done, and do, very well in this area. There are many good examples to learn from. We also have a community network for public health. However, for the future this should not be based solely on local fires and enthusiastic employees in the municipal sector. They become very important in the future too, but now all the municipalities and municipalities of the country have a responsibility, and not least the state as well. The Government shall follow the Public Health Act with an implementation plan. It shall include economic instruments, the development of a better knowledge base and the enhancement of competences. This work shall be carried out across departments and across all sectors. I would like to stress that this law and also what it says about public health at national health level is a first important step on the way. Responsibility is placed, and the challenges are described. But the follow-up to this work to be done by the government will be very important if the public health effort is to get that promise across the country that we now expect. In the end, I just want to refer to the two proposals for amendments to the law, before I end up as a case rapporteur and go over to my post on the other laws as an SV representative. Let me first express my joy at the breakthrough we are making today for people with disabilities. Finally, we take a decision that obliges the government to come back with a proposal for a right-holding, and in the case of BPA, the system of user-led personal assistant. I will commend everyone who has been patient and worked towards this goal. SV has a long tradition of supporting liberation movements, and the fight that people with disabilities fight is such a struggle. We politicians have a responsibility to back it up. The right-wing of BPA will be such a contribution. I am very pleased that we are going to make a decision today. Since the Health Inspectorate recently supervised the follow-up to the Law on Social Services in Nav, supervision found direct offences in 45 out of 50 municipalities. It's very serious. It has been important for us to ensure good complaint procedures, and we have come up with a good solution, which ensures the right to appeal and access of the review bodies to reverse decisions. The government has taken up much of the criticism that came through the consultation round against the original proposal. For SV, I would like to stress that it is important to strengthen legal certainty, especially for the most vulnerable groups. I believe we have now. Recruitment of doctors to municipal health and specialist health services throughout the country and all its municipalities are becoming very important in the coming years. It is therefore natural for it to be reacted when such a successful scheme as the Tournament Scheme is to be revised. It's being reacted because the lottery system that's been practiced has largely laid the groundwork for recruiting newly-trained doctors to the whole country. In the vote, a joint committee states that any change in the medical rotation service must not be at the expense of recruitment and medical coverage in the districts. It's important. The quality of the Tournament system for both medical training and, not least, for the recruitment of doctors to the whole country must be reflected in the new system that the government is now working on. The new law merges the old social service law with the municipal health law. There is expressed concern that the social responsibility of the law is weakened. I understand that concern, but I am also aware that it is not the intention of the legislator to weaken it. Changes have therefore been made after the consultation round. The municipalities still have a clear responsibility to capture those who will fall outside, and that is a very important part of the welfare state we are now talking about. I would like to underline the purpose of the law as, among other things, stresses that it should ‘encourage social protection, improve living conditions for disadvantaged people, contribute to equality and equality and prevent social problems’; This is a heavy responsibility, a responsibility I expect the municipalities to take the most seriously, even in the future. In the national health plan, local hospitals have received a special mention. A minimum level of local hospital is described. Local hospitals shall have the specialist health service of the day, as a minimum, in the field of internal medicine and the competence to carry out simple day-to-day surgical procedures. Furthermore, in areas with long distances and difficult communication conditions, there is a particular need to be able to deal with acute conditions. The majority of the Committee has also added that there must be anaesthetic preparedness in these local hospitals. One would think that anaesthesia was a matter of course when it came to emergency preparedness, but it is not. It turns out that it is planned, and it has been planned, local hospital with emergency care without anaesthesia. That is why this emphasis is included. With regard to the focus in the specialist healthcare services for others and in hospitals in particular, patient safety and quality are the main focus. Despite the fact that we have a health service and a specialist health service that is ranked at the top of the international level, mistakes are made. Patient safety is not always good enough, and there is a critical searchlight on the quality systems in our hospitals. The director of the Health Authority also says clearly that the hospitals are too poor to learn from errors that are being made. Errors are repeated, and it is a symptom that the quality systems are not good enough. The government has notified a major report on patient safety. I welcome it and hope it will be widely developed. I believe that patient safety needs to be assessed in a broad perspective. We need to look at how the management systems work. Security and quality must be given the highest priority. So finally just a few words: we don't make it all over with short speaking time. In the health plan, the government gives the starting signal for what eventually has to become a dental reform. The teeth should be part of the body, as it is called. Now we're going to figure out how this is going to happen. Once in the future, I believe that dental health must become a part of popular health as other health is. It seems like a remarkable thing that we don't have dental health fully inside the population. It is my and SV's goal that we should come closer to the goal of the investigation that is being carried out.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e499	The issues we have to deal with today are a further follow-up to the big message that Stortinget dealt with last year. First, I would like to take the opportunity to thank the rapporteurs for their excellent work and for their good cooperation in the committee, which has made it possible, within a difficult time frame, to deal with such major issues as such, not least changes in the law. What one looks like from the settings that are on the table today is that the consensus that the Party of Advancement, the Christian People's Party, the Left and Right established in the context of the handling of the paper on the cooperation reform last spring has continued in the matters that are also linked to the legislative changes that these four parties are united in. It is also worth remembering that it was a wish of these parties last spring that one should get to a debate with the government parties on such a great health reform as this was initially supposed to, where one could ensure Norway a broader political basis for that reform than what one now sees. I noted last spring that the government parties did not want negotiations, and we can also note that, through the consideration of these three major issues, there have been no approaches from the government parties linked to getting a broad settlement in these matters. But at least we see that the consensus established in the opposition is still at hand. Nevertheless, it means that there is a health reform here, where a government shift in 2013 can also affect the content of the reform. It is a paradox that we have a government that asks the healthcare community to interact, to engage in healthcare interactions, but that is not able to interact with healthcare itself. In the three major issues we are dealing with today, one will see that there are three main differences in the approach to this reform. The opposition and the government parties all agree that this is the right direction to go for the Norwegian health service, but the instruments disagree. First, it's about the way you think about reform. One sees the majority putting up for a top-down reform, where one steps a template across the country for a reform to be carried out at record speed, while the opposition is more concerned that this could become a bottom-up reform, where a lot of service evolves locally and not least through different types of experiment. Testing different models in different parts of the country means that we would be much wiser than implementing a model for everyone in such a short time. We also have extensive experience with this from other reforms, not least in the health sector, where one sees that implementation, for example. of the fast-track reform went very well precisely because a different type of approach was chosen, while one in other reforms, such as the Nav reform, has chosen this industry and seen its results. The third main difference is also linked to the funding model, where one sees that the majority are consistently very concerned that this should be a zero-sum game in which one transfers money from the specialist health service to the community health service and believes that it should be the way to go to build this reform, not least through complicated and bureaucratic funding models. In the three major cases, the new national health and care plan, municipal health and care law and public health law, I will briefly explain what are the main options of the Right. There will also be alternatives to which we stand with the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left. In our view, the national health and care plan should also have been a national operational hospital plan, which contained a description of what should be the minimum offer in the different types of hospitals, and not just a description of what should be a minimum offer in a local hospital. There would have been a lot of discussion about what we see very many people are concerned about: how the hospital structure in Norway should look: away from businesses and into elected bodies, into the Storting, where responsibility belongs. In the final phase of the treatment of the national health and care plan, a discussion was held in which government parties entered and made a change related to anaesthesia. It clearly shows that the definition of the content of the local hospital government is not based on a good process where professional recommendations end up with political balances, but where one in late night hours, as we have been illustrated clearly on television, sits and flashes back and forth in political decision-making processes in order to achieve political objectives linked to a statement that everyone sees cracking in total. There is a wrong process related to how vital functions towards hospital content should be established. Here should have had a national health plan that was based on good professional advice, good local processes and then a political balance at the end of the year in Stortinget. Now the government says that this should also be anchored in regulations. There is a review in this law that many important decisions are now being moved from the Storting and into regulatory processes. The opposition points out that interaction is not only about interconnection of services but also about major challenges related to the lack of services. In particular, in the context of the national health and care plan, a stepping-up plan is proposed in the area of rehabilitation and a stepping-up plan in the field of drug use. The new municipal health and care law is a combination of the old municipal health law and the social services law. The right here suggests, together with the Party of Advancement and the Christian People's Party, that the name of the law should continue to contain the term "social". It is essential, because this is not only a health and care law, it should also be a social law. In fact, many people are born into this law and, throughout their lives, need services that are anchored in it – without necessarily being patients or having a need for care, even if they need this type of service. In this context, the concept of care is, to a large extent, a top-down concept in relation to the social concept. That is why it is of great symbolic importance. The government parties propose municipal co-financing. The proposed municipal co-financing, based on diagnosis, will involve real age discrimination in the Norwegian health system. Many were happy when the government went out and said it dropped the proposal to introduce an 80-year rule into the law. But what it has come back with is, in fact, the same thing, because the medical diagnosis on which municipal co-financing is to be based is essentially medical diagnosis that affects the elderly. The law and argument about this is based on the belief that too many older people obviously come too quickly into hospitals. This is not a professional basis to claim. The four-year commitment of the Health Authority to the supervision of services for the elderly, which was reported in 2010, shows the very opposite, namely that hospitalisation directly in hospitals and not through hospitals, where diagnostic competence exists, was cited as a concrete example of failure. The supervisory experience of the Health Authority in individual cases also shows that too many elderly people do not receive a quick enough offer of hospitalisation. The government’s own expert group on elderly people in healthcare, which was established in the context of this reform, came to exactly the same conclusion: it is a challenge for older people to be late for hospitalisation. The introduction of municipal co-financing for elderly people entering hospitals will in reality build up the barrier for this patient group to enter hospitals. There is direct age discrimination, which is now incorporated into the law. We're against that. The representative Sonja Irene Sjøli will get into our view of professional neutrality, which is another area where community health services are weakened, as a result of the law passed today.

The Christian People's Party believes that it is necessary to identify the need for sick homes in order to achieve full health care coverage in 2015. Today’s investment is based on service provision covering the needs of the elderly, but reports show that, at present, there is already an unmet need among the sickest of the elderly. As a result, other parts of the supply chain are under increased stress and quality is reduced. In addition, not many nursing homes have benefited from new jobs in recent years, despite the fact that the sickest of older people live here. Most new jobs in the health and care sector have gone to younger people under the age of 67 and to home services, who also needed them. The incidence of dementia is increasing with increasing age, but younger people down to the age of 30 to 40 may also develop dementia. A dementia diagnosis affects the whole family in a demanding way. Therefore, good, comprehensive services at all stages of the disease are essential for health and quality of life, including for relatives. The Christian People's Party will point out in particular the need to legislate the daily activity offer of home-dwelling denments now, and not wait until 2015 as the government puts it. We will also point out the need to organise housing services with activities that provide recognition, security and bring about the ‘golden moments’, as well as ensuring quality and competence in dementia care, and that working methods that provide security, well-being, mastering and dignity for individuals are used. Fixed meeting places for the elderly are so important that the Christian People's Party will legislate on the provision of elderly centres in the Health and Care Services Act. The organisation may be different, either under the direction of non-governmental organisations or in cooperation with municipalities. The Christian People's Party is very disappointed that the government is training the matter of user-driven personal assistant and does not use the opportunity to legislate this offer now. The deferral of the law is justified by a report on the cost calculations for the municipalities, which must be seen as a substitute argument, and which the government has had nearly six years to figure out. As early as 2005, the Health Minister, Sylvia Brustad, promised to follow up the Stortingen’s decision on the right to BPA. It's about helping to make the practical part of everyday life work – things that we go through in turbo travel, take for granted. It's also about dignity, respect and participation. A young BPA user said it so aptly: User-controlled personal assistant is for me my arms and feet. Finally, a little bit about another big and controversial relationship, namely the proposal for professional neutrality. The Christian People's Party experiences at the border of self-contradictors when it is called upon to take more responsibility for patient treatment — which requires more competence and more professionals — while at the same time introducing a profession-neutral law that will just weaken the requirements for competence. We believe that more stringent requirements need to be imposed on the competence of municipalities in order to ensure good health care. In addition to doctors, it must be, among other things: nurses, midwives, physiotherapists, psychologists and other health professionals. After massive protests, the government has proposed a law where they cannot, should not, give regulations, but there is nothing in the attitude of the government parties that suggests that this is imminent.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e857	In our lifetime, there have been dramatic changes in the treatment of a number of diseases. This means that the healthcare system needs to adapt, so that we can always offer the best possible treatment. A few decades ago, if you had a heart attack, there was little to be done. Now the diagnosis can be made in ambulances or helicopters, and if necessary the blood clot can be removed in one of the hospitals that has the type of treatment. At the same time, knowledge of prevention has increased and mortality due to cardiovascular diseases is finally falling. With increasing life expectancy, more people are affected by cancer, but there are also more who survive the disease. We know that more people are affected by diseases like COPD, diabetes 2, and muscle and skeletal disorders, and that we're getting older. Therefore, we need to improve the treatment of patients with such chronic disorders who need follow-up over time, and often from several instances — not only to treat them when acute illness occurs. Changes in the picture of the disease and demographic trends make it necessary to change health and care services. We still have to change to improve. It's been two years since we left St.meld. No 47 for 2008-2009, the Cooperation Reform, which described the direction and content of the reform. In April, we presented two legislative proposals and a national health and care plan, and now we are going to launch the Interaction Reform. Everyone agrees that it is better to prevent disease from occurring than to repair it — mainly for the individual, but a healthier population is also good for society. That is why we should focus on promoting health, good prevention and close follow-up of those who get sick. Interaction reform is both a policy reform and a long-term development programme to ensure quality and sustainability. The reform is already under way. Now we come up with the tools: economic, legal, organisational and professional. It's the sum of the instruments that change the price, and we're moving gradually and properly. A larger proportion of the growth in health budgets will come in municipalities in the next few years. We will follow the implementation closely in the future. It will pay off for municipalities to invest in public health. Preventing disease and building up own offerings shall be worthwhile. By means of municipal co-financing, municipalities will be given greater responsibility for the health of their citizens. They have to pay part of the bill when people are hospitalized, so that municipalities are aware of the costs of treatment, and that alternative use of money can promote health, prevent disease and provide equally good or better treatment where people live. The municipalities will be transferred more than a 4 billion. from hospitals to pay their share of these costs. Many outpatients stay in hospitals for too long. It's not good for the patient, and it's precious for the community. Therefore, municipalities must assume financial responsibility for patients who are ready to print from the first day. 560 million. kr shall be transferred to the municipalities. The money will be an incentive to make good deals for these patients. Let me make it quite clear: the cooperation reform will provide better treatment for all patients. Of course, anyone who needs specialist health care should still get it. It is neither the councilman nor the chief economic officer nor the rapporteur in a municipality who decides when patients are going to hospital. It's like before the doctor's decision applies. Many hospitalisations today can offer equally good or better alternatives through municipalities. At the same time, building up the competences of municipalities will be important for those who need to go to the hospital to actually get it. For some patient groups it is particularly important to get to a specialist quickly. This applies in particular to, for example: if serious cancer is suspected. In order to ensure rapid diagnosis and treatment of these patients, indicative deadlines are now being introduced for the different parts of the course of treatment. From 2016, all municipalities will be required to establish immediate assistance as a 24-hour stay for patients in need of treatment or observation. Some municipalities have already made such offers, for example: at the Fozen District Medical Centre. COLLEU patients are among those who get a safe and good offer there, which makes them more likely to drop hospitalisation. The new Health and Welfare Act clarifies a common value base for the entire municipal health and care service. We get a coordinated law, and the law has to ensure that interaction becomes a reality. In addition, amendments to a number of other laws are proposed to facilitate interaction. Municipalities and hospitals are required to enter into an agreement on interaction and resolution in several areas. A goal will be to avoid insertion if the patient can get an equal or better offer in the municipality. Who hasn't heard of the kidney disease patient who has to travel miles to the hospital? The cooperation agreements between hospitals and municipalities will address this issue. Patients should not be allowed to go to hospitals for treatment that they may be given as an alternative in their local environment. The law strengthens the position of patients and users. It shall ensure that they participate in the design of the service offer to a much greater extent than at present. Everyone wants to make a living. Those who are users of healthcare services have experience, knowledge and opinions that are essential for their further development. The law is designed in a general and professionally neutral way. This has been done precisely because municipalities should have the freedom to develop their services in line with local conditions and needs. Municipalities are different, the need for competence and capacity varies. We've taken that into account. But we make clear demands on the quality of the law, and the responsibility for ensuring responsibility is made clear. The Public Health Act is about thinking about health in everything we do, locally, municipally and nationally. Public health work shall be anchored in municipal and county plans to ensure long-term and systematic work. Municipalities shall use all sectors and instruments to promote the health of their citizens. The municipality itself shall have an overview of its own public health challenges, while the State shall be responsible for providing data and making it available to the municipalities. When we know the challenges and causes, we can take action that promotes health and balances social health differences. In order to achieve the objective of safety for all, the quality of health and care services in the future needs to be good. If we are to succeed, we need to train, recruit, develop and maintain competent personnel. The government has clearly shown that we are following up on the investment in several annual works in the municipalities. The number of persons employed in the care sector is increasing, and the target of 12 000 new annual works by 2015 is therefore within reach. We are currently legislating that all establishments in the local and specialist health services are to carry out systematic work for quality improvement and patient safety. I want to follow up on these issues in my own big message, which the government is going to promote during 2012. So to the claim of haste. I dare remind you that the work on the cooperation reform has been going on for more than three years – yes, even longer if we look at all the samples that have actually worked on the preparation for this. We have had an open and inclusive process along the way, with hearings, public meetings and meetings with municipalities and organisations. We've had a close cooperation with KS. We have received a lot of input, and we have developed the tools in dialogue with key actors. In political debates, we are often more concerned with disagreement than with consensus. I suppose that's the way it might have been in this case. It is a full opening-up to the Reform of Cooperation as an idea, and there is also a broad consensus on the main actions. It'll be heard here today, too. Then there is some disagreement about some of the instruments. This government does not just have the ideas for the cooperation reform. We'll implement them, too. While the right-hand side here will go forward slowly, the government is rather impatient on behalf of the patients. I want to create systems, so healthcare interacts better, and so patients get better follow-up. It's urgent. But we haven't had a hurry with the tools. They are thoroughly and well thought out, and we know from experience that hospitals and municipalities can interact. The interaction reform is about turning the effort more against prevention and thinking a little differently. We get far on purpose and commitment, but we also have to have systems that support a different way of thinking. That is what the means of reform are for.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e1099	The proposal for a new municipal health and care law clarifies the overall responsibility of municipalities to provide health and care services to the population. The obligation of municipalities continues, but is proposed to be more comprehensive and professionally neutral. The government does not want to bind municipalities with specific organisational or personnel solutions. Therefore, no guidance has been provided as to what competence is required to perform the various services or who is required to assess what competence is required. The Government considers that several occupational groups can carry out many of the same services. For the Right, it is essential that the services provided are of high quality and that they are provided as needed and in a timely manner. We are, however, in the same way as the State Health Authority, the Norwegian Association of Nurses, the Norwegian Association of midwives, the Joint Organisation of Disabled Persons and many other hearing bodies are deeply concerned that professional neutrality can create uncertainties about what is sufficient competence in the performance of certain services. The municipalities already have demanding professional tasks, the municipalities are very different, and the cooperation reform will increase tasks both in scope and complexity. It is therefore essential to ensure the quality of healthcare services. The right considers that the considerations of legal certainty and equivalence provide for a certain level of government governance and that the requirement of soundness alone will not be sufficient to maintain the quality of the provision of local authorities at a sound and desirable level. The legislative proposal, in its neutral form, may lead to municipalities recruiting personnel without the necessary professional competence in health and care services. This is a development Right cannot accept. There are certain occupational groups on which municipal health and care services are dependent in order to provide professionally sound services. The right proposes, together with the Christian People's Party and the Advance Party, to include several professional groups and that the legal text § 3-2 first paragraph no. 4 is replaced by the following: ‘Examining, diagnosis and treatment, including fixed medical devices, psychological services, nursing, physiotherapy and midwifery services.’ The mention of these groups does not, of course, restrict the use of other occupational groups by municipalities in order to meet health and care tasks. The municipalities have no legal obligation today to provide psychological services, but we believe that this is a necessary clarification both as a consequence of the escalation plan for mental health and in view of the tasks that local authorities will now be responsible for in implementing the cooperation reform. We believe that a plan is needed to ensure the psychological competence of the municipalities. Municipalities need the necessary professional skills both for the services to be provided and for assessing what is lacking in competence to solve the health and care tasks. The Government and Government Parties consider professional neutrality to be beneficial in promoting the composition of professional competences across professionals and sub-services, and that professional neutrality would discourage the appearance of others as more important than others. If the ideology is that it's not important who should do it, just the job is done, the Right believes it's a dangerous way to go. It may reduce the need for high levels of competence in nursing and other health professions and poorer quality of health and care services for the population. Already today we know that over 30 pst. of those working in the care sector in municipalities are unskilled. Some disciplines are more important than others in a specific situation, depending on competence, role, function and, in particular, patients’ needs. The right believes that good interaction requires that individuals know their own subject, are clear on their own professional strength and pay attention to the limitations of the subject. The key to professional and multidisciplinary professional work is knowledge, respect and flexibility in interaction. Right-hand is against professional neutrality, because we believe that professional neutrality is impossible. The actions of professionals are always based on a value base, although values vary from person to person. Let me use the hospital as an example. All people exercise different forms of care, but nurses are responsible for professionally exercising health-promoting and rehabilitating care functions. We also know that poor nursing skills are now a challenge in municipal health services. We believe that professional neutrality will water down a completely necessary interaction in the health and care services in the municipalities. If everything is neutral, there will be no specific knowledge to add and no one to interact with. When professional neutrality is emphasised, structures will be controlled even more strongly. Maybe that's what the government wants. It is, of course, the link between professional knowledge and soundness that is essential for the quality of the services provided to users. Professional neutrality will primarily be able to affect those patient groups on which there is a strong focus in the cooperation reform, namely those with complex and complex needs for assistance and a high need for coordinated services, as well as those who will benefit from increased prevention and health promotion efforts. Good individual health services, good interaction between service levels and around individual patients are ensured by professional competence in professionals. Appropriate and financial organisation must not be at the expense of soundness. A clear professional identity and integrity is a prerequisite for good services in municipalities. The sum of safe professional identity provides interdisciplinary cooperation for the benefit of users. Keeping us more healthy when we get – more – older will also have a benefit for society, but all of us, most of all for the individual, who get better quality of life and a better life.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e1296	Today’s debate includes the proposed representative in Document 8:130 S for 2010–2011, which calls for the introduction of a so-called ‘packaging’ process in cancer treatment in Norway, following a model from Denmark. As rapporteur on this subject, I shall begin with it in my speech. The Danish 48-hour cancer treatment model operates with a deadline which includes an investigation to be conducted within 48 hours and a plan for its implementation. The silver investigation period will naturally vary, because different diagnostics require tests of different durations. Therefore, it is important to take into account the entire course of patients, from referral from regular to initiation of treatment and with clear targets for time use related to the individual types of cancer. In Denmark, the ‘package course’ model was introduced after several years of criticism of long waiting times in cancer treatment. Last year’s figures were actually lower in Denmark than in Norway for several serious cancers when measuring the median waiting time from referral to treatment initiation. Denmark still has a lower survival rate after cancer than other Nordic countries. In 2010, the State Health Authority pointed out that the level of risk in Norwegian cancer treatment is too high. This has to do with several aspects – such as late diagnosis, poor flow of information and the lack of coordination between the different actors. It is therefore very important for the Minister of Health and Welfare to inform the Health Directorate earlier that the national guidelines for the diagnosis, treatment and follow-up of cancer patients should be reviewed. The aim is to introduce maximum time limits for each of the different stages of the investigation and treatment of cancer patients in all health establishments. More money for patient treatment, more quality indicators and clear deadlines for the different parts of the patient’s course are important pillars of government policy. Good information on actual waiting times, where the figures from regional health establishments are regularly updated, is another important element of the requirements that the Ministry of Health and Health now imposes on health businesses. Several regional health businesses have started work on good and effective patient trajectories, so that unnecessary waiting can be eliminated. The University Hospital of North Norway has launched an improvement in the effectiveness of patient progression for several cancers, including lung cancer and cancer of the stomach and intestines. Compared time use before and after the introduction of a complete patient cycle shows significant improvements and less variation in patient waiting time – despite the fact that the University Hospital North Norway, after the functional distribution in Health Nord in 2010, received more cancer patients belonging to the regional health company than it had before. It shows that much can be achieved by entering into and improving the organisation of patient processes themselves, without necessarily requiring an increased financial framework. There has recently been a strong public searchlight for patient injury and patient safety. It's important. The first patient injury report from the Norwegian health care system came in 2004 and was carried out by Professor Peter F. Hjort, who then estimated that approx. 15,000 people were injured annually in the healthcare system, and as many as 2,000 were likely to die. That's unacceptable numbers. That's why the work this government is doing is so important. This work includes the new guidelines for waiting times in cancer treatment and related new quality indicators. The large patient safety campaign In safe hands was launched by the Minister of Health in January this year and involves all health businesses. Work needs to be targeted both at individual and system level to increase safety and to make the health sector a learning organisation. We need to have good reporting systems and a good follow-up of adverse and serious events. A call-out group under the authority of the State Health Authority for immediate action in the event of serious incidents or death in the healthcare system was established last year and shall be evaluated after two years. Out of the national patient safety campaign, a national set of patient safety indicators shall be developed, which shall enable an ongoing evaluation. I'm glad that this government will not be able to present the first major patient safety message in a long time. I think that will provide a good basis for the important work we have to do in this field in the years to come.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e1506	The interaction reform is a reform that can be described as a reversal of the health supply in Norway. The biggest change is the organisation of health services, where more responsibilities and tasks are transferred from regional health enterprises down to municipalities. I agree with the principle of the Cooperation Reform, but there are a number of issues that remain unanswered. In order to meet the new tasks of the Interaction Reform, municipalities need to build up both facilities and competences, and this will result in considerable expenditure. Even today, many municipalities have to make economic considerations more important than quality and professional standards of services, because of the pressure of local economy. Among other things, the Interaction Reform will oblige municipalities to provide day-to-day care services for patients and users in need of immediate assistance. This will involve considerable costs for municipalities, and it is therefore important – as several have been involved – that this scheme be fully financed. In addition, it must be demonstrated that the municipalities have the necessary professional skills and capacity to handle immediate assistance, daily subsistence, in a good way. Interaction reform also provides for professional neutrality. I'm skeptical of such an approach. In the worst case, due to its neutral form, the legislative proposal may lead to municipalities recruiting personnel without the necessary professional competence in the health and care services. Therefore, it is important to continue a legal requirement on certain key prostheses, as well as on fixed doctors, in municipal health and care services. The report is made from a selection where all members have an interest in running the cost calculations. In the report, there is a one-sided approach to the costs of municipalities and not to the benefits of society. In Sweden, the country co-finances the costs of BPA in such a way that municipalities have an interest in applying for this scheme as much as possible with assistance needs. There would probably be a good solution in Norway too. Finally, it is appropriate to ask the following questions: How many municipalities are really clear when the reform comes into force in less than half a year? What happens to the municipalities that are following? It is important once again to point out that this is a reform that involves changes in all municipalities throughout the country – small and large. Such a reform must be implemented at the appropriate pace. Many municipalities feel that this is being dragged down their heads now, and it serves neither the municipalities, the patients, nor the reform as such.

The freedom of the municipalities at this point is restricted and the obligations of the municipalities are increasing.» Thus, today’s legal status is changed when the term ‘worthiness’ is highlighted in the Patient Rights Act in accordance with the proposal for the provisions on health care provisions in the Health and Care Services Act § 4-1. A little jus, but quite identical to what we agreed with the government about when Bjarne Håkon Hanssen was the Council of State. Local hospitals with emergency functions must have at least – and I stress the minimum – hospital emergency services as well as simpler day-to-day surgical procedures. So this is not the golden rule, but the minimum competence of state institutions that can be called hospitals. I would also like to point out that it should be possible to ensure that this is done, that a change in the management model of hospitals is made so that every hospital is managed locally. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e1909	The matter we are dealing with today concerns a proposal from the Progress Party to establish a heart bridge for Denmark for ablation spaces, and that the work on building up the capacity of the Feiring Clinic is intensified. The proposers claim that it will take a long time to build up the offer in Norway and that on the basis of this, a heart bridge has to be established for Denmark. Heart fibrillation reduces the quality of life for those who are afflicted with it and can lead to a stroke. Therefore, it is important that we have good responses to this growing patient group. The Health Directorate estimates that 4 pst. heart fibrillation should be the main measure of ablation treatment. The Minister for Health has for a long time focused on this issue and has, in letters to regional health companies in June 2010, instructed them to extend the offer to this patient group. The Health Directorate has recommended a six-month period for those patients who have been assessed for need of treatment. That is a recommendation and, of course, an individual assessment must be made which may result in the patient being treated more quickly for reasons of safety. The Ministry has assessed that although regional health businesses have developed plans to increase capacity, there will be too little capacity in the public health sector in an interim period. The Minister of Health has therefore, by letter to the regional health establishments, in the autumn of 2010, made it clear that, if health businesses do not have the capacity to offer treatment in their own healthcare undertakings, they shall ensure that the patient is offered treatment through agreements with other service providers, in order to comply with the requirement of safety. The Minister for Health has also made it clear that all patients in need of ablation should be assessed in relation to the prioritisation and the requirement for health care. Health enterprises have been required to report on this work in the mission document and the reports will be reviewed and followed up in the reporting meetings with regional health enterprises now in June. We know that several healthcare companies have increased their capacity for ablation treatment. Health South-East has signed an agreement with the Feiring Clinic, and we know that other health regions consider doing the same. It is serious when patients with severe distress and risk have to wait beyond the recommended deadline. For this reason, I am pleased that the Minister of Health, in his letter to the Committee, stresses that if health businesses do not provide a satisfactory offer to patients in need of ablation treatment, the Minister will follow it up separately with the individual enterprise.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2085	Since the Stortinget is going to deal with the revised national budget in its entirety on Friday, I intend to keep today's debate to a minimum. When the Committee deals with the budgets, it's sometimes hectic, and there's a lot of elements to keep track of, so I want to start by thanking the Committee for good cooperation. That thank-you must also be shared with the employees of Stortinget and the party groups. I would like to begin by saying that there is a broad consensus about the proposals that the government has made in the revised national budget for the legislative changes. I should therefore only say a little about three of the issues that there has been the most attention around. The first thing is probably the one that got the most space in the media, and that's been discussed several times in this room. It's about how to publish the tax lists. The government is adding up to a tightening of the practice you've had. The new system adds that everyone can now search the tax registers by logging into my ID port. When you get in, you can only apply for one single name in the tax list. Furthermore, it is stated that the media can obtain an electronic copy of the entire tax list if they sign an agreement that they will not publish all or part of the list and that they cannot otherwise disclose it in whole or in part. This seeks to ensure the essential function of the media as a societally critical actor towards both authorities and societal development. The proposal does not intend to limit journalism by itself, but the way in which information is disseminated. The proposal will also prevent the development of the commercialisation of tax lists that have been seen in recent years. There has been a development that has greatly contributed to an increased pressure on privacy, to which all parties have expressed scepticism. The Government's proposals are supported by a majority of the Committee, while a varying minority have different proposals that will further reinforce current practice. The second issue I would like to make is the depreciation rules for operating funds in industrial plants. This was also one of the main concerns of Norwegian Industry during the consultation of the Committee. Over time, there has been a trend in this area, both because new balancing groups have been created, so that there are now ten different groups in which depreciation can be classified, and because new ways of constructing buildings and installations are constantly beyond the challenges of what is to be defined as what. The consequence of this has been that the legal system also has problems distinguishing between what is to be considered as machinery and what is to be considered as plant. This is a problem with which the majority is well aware, and the budget for the current year also has a majority view on this. The majority therefore look forward to the announcement by the government that there will be a review of next year's budget in this area. The third thing I want to say a little about is the changes you add to the inheritance tax law. There have been several changes in this law over time to equalise spouses and cohabitants. These have been important and correct changes supported by a broad majority in the United Kingdom. The changes that are now being made also have broad support. In short, there has been an inequality in today's legislation due to the fact that spouses have been able to stay in the same place with their heirs without the inheritance tax obligation being lifted before either distributions from the residence or the termination of the change. This possibility has not been able to benefit cohabiters in the same way. The government's proposal now changes this, so that there is equal treatment in this area between spouses and cohabitants. It is also proposed that a change in the amount of the allowance be made in the case of a distribution from a change of residence, so that distribution from the residence can now be calculated as coming from both spouses, or from the consensual spouses. This means, in practice, that you can now count two free amounts and not one as in today's system. This is a grip that makes it easier for families to transfer values to grandchildren. I will finally make an oral correction. In the Committee's recommendation, a letter has been included too much under Decision A and Decision B. Under Roman numerals I and ‘The title of the Law’ is replaced by ‘In Law 19 June 1964, paragraphs 19 and 64 respectively. 14...’ and ‘In Law 12 December 1975 No. 59...’. Instead, it shall read ‘Law...’ without ‘I’ in front of both places. I therefore ask that the recommendation be made in line with this. With this, I recommend that Stortinget support the majority position.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2114	There is a lot about the Norwegian tax system that is unique. As the only country in Europe, we choose to punish owners of businesses that create jobs in the private sector. We do this very much by seizing the working capital of businesses every year – capital used by companies in our competitors to create new and competitive jobs. We're not doing this because as a nation, we need this money for welfare services. No, this money collects the state so that our surplus on the state budget can increase accordingly. Such a policy is called responsible. And it becomes even more responsible when this surplus is invested in our competitors' businesses through the State pension fund abroad. Of course, these companies invested on the assumption that we should not exert influence over the operation of these companies. Another unique tax phenomenon in Norway is that we publish and make entertainment of people's incomes and what people pay in taxes. There is a sense of responsibility in the system, because in this way we can all be each other's monitors. The government now wants to change how people's income, wealth and tax payments are published. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2264	The VAT is designed as a general consumption charge, which should ideally have a broad basis, so that different types of consumption are treated in the same way as possible. This will ensure a level playing field and prevent the increase in distortions, adaptations and administrative costs. Both new technologies and increased trade between countries are a development we want. We can download more and more electronic products, for example. music, books and movies, via the Internet. It's a pleasure and gives us as consumers new opportunities. The VAT base in Norway is narrower than among our main trading partners. Through the decision of the Storting on the 2011 tax regime, it became clear that we impose VAT on electronic services purchased from abroad by individuals in Norway, in the same way as in the EU, so that trade in electronic services is treated in a non-discriminatory manner regardless of whether the seller is in Norway or abroad. It is important both to ensure the principle of non-discrimination between Norwegian and foreign providers and to prevent the VAT gap from becoming too large when the number of products with tax exemptions is growing. The decision does not imply the charging of a new type of service, but the payment of VAT on the purchase of such services irrespective of whether the provider is established outside or within the Norwegian VAT range. The setting we have for treatment today is a follow-up to this decision. The government announced the time when there would be a need to establish a simplified registration system for providers from abroad, so that operators, for example: will not require a representative in Norway to register. This is in line with the regime in the EU countries. I am pleased that a broad majority of the Committee, the Government Parties, the Right and the Christian People's Party support the proposal for a simplified registration and reporting system and that the system should be in place when the fee obligation enters into force on 1 July. The new system will ensure that there is a law that is easy to comply with while removing a distortion of the VAT system. The simplified system includes, inter alia,: a voluntary alternative to registration in the VAT register, electronic communications via Altin between providers and charging authorities, electronic communications with a single tax office, simplified registration procedures, simplified reporting and simplified documentation requirements. The registration limit will normally be NOK 50000. As the proposal is very similar to the rules adopted by EU Member States since 2003, several of those affected by the proposal will already be aware of its content. The minority of the Committee, the Progress Party and the Left, oppose the proposal for a simple reporting system and justify it, inter alia: with regard to these products, their resistance to the obligation of VAT itself. This obligation was thus adopted in the past, in the context of the 2011 tax and tax package, and when that debate was decided, it would be nice if we could agree to establish a simple registration mechanism. Given that the VAT decision remains in place – and there has not been a rematch – the consequence of going against the new regime is that the old, less light-minded rules remain. With that background, I think it is a relatively simple decision to vote in favour of the proposal, which provides a simpler system. But I'm glad that a broad majority is behind the vote. And we'll probably hear more about the rationale for the minority's view of the posts that are coming.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2357	The store is today invited to make two changes to the Gambling Act. In addition to the audit mission, the Riksrevision carries out company and management control of the company. In the period around 2007 there was some noise around Norwegian Typing.
