In connection with the prohibition that non-Norwegian Typing should be able to set up vending machines entered into force on 1 July 2007, a derogation from this ban was made by the fact that bingo machines could still be privately operated in bingo halls. We recorded a significant increase in the turnover of bingo vending machines between 2006 and 2009. In parallel with this, a large increase was recorded in the number of requests to the Game Dependent Guide. It was therefore decided that bingo vending machines were also banned from 1 April 2010 and that these bingo vending machines could be replaced by their own bingo vending machines from Norsk Typing AS. A profit is expected on the operation of Norwegian Typing bingo terminals. The question we have to address is how to distribute this surplus after Norwegian Typing and the bingo contractors have received their compensation. With the government's proposal, the profit after the distribution will be included in the key. Norwegian Typing estimates that this will be 50 million. kr in the start-up wounds. The majority support the government in this. The challenge has been to identify the level of turnover that should be the basis for the distribution of the surplus. The Government’s proposal to base its 2006 turnover is supported by the majority of the Committee. That means when the total bingo game in the whole country exceeds 127 million. kr, the profits of Norwegian Typing's winning terminals shall be included in the key. The majority also agree with the government that the distribution key should be distributed in the first years so that Norwegian Typing is left with 40 pst, while the remaining one is distributed among bingo contractors with 35 pst. and layers and associations of 25 pst. I think it might be good to clarify that when this distribution comes into force, it's the turnover of each bingo room to be taken into account, so that everyone gets the part of the turnover they're supposed to have. It is also important to clarify that such a key should be adjusted by means of a separate regulation in the years following the introduction of this amendment. Gambling policies are challenging in many ways. One side is that it provides good income for teams and organisations, but it also has social challenges such as: the risk of gambling dependence. And finally, the majority of the committee agrees that it was important to limit the large increase we have seen in electronic games in bingo halls in recent years. Finding the balance point between these two considerations is difficult, but through what is being presented today, we believe that we have come up with solutions that ensure both of these considerations in a good way by offering the Belago machines from Norwegian Typing and certain restrictions on the data bingo terminals. Finally, this case has been committed, and it was just supposed to be missing. But I think it's important to clarify that in the future, volunteering will also have good income opportunities through its bingo activities. All turnover in traditional bingo and data bingo games will be attributed to organisers and teams/organisations as at present. In addition, those who choose it will also receive their income from Norwegian Typing bingo machines. Strengthening the pot for gambling through this legislative change further helps to facilitate a better framework for volunteering in Norway.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2436	This case concerns a new statutory audit scheme for Norsk Tipping AS and the distribution of profits from bingo terminals. Briefly on the first new statutory audit system: the legislative proposal involves a breakdown of the audit tasks, so that the Riksaudition carries out the company and management audit with Norsk Tipping AS, while a selected external auditor carries out the ongoing audit of the company’s accounts. The Christian People's Party considers this to be a strengthening of the control of Norwegian Typing. So to the issue of distribution of profits from bingo terminals: Bingo operations in Norway are currently made up of both traditional paper-based bingo games and electronic machines. Recently there has been a significant increase in the use of electronic terminals at the expense of the paper product. We also see that the age structure in the bingo rooms has changed, and the electronic machines attract younger age groups. It is important to dampen the growth of electronic services, which may cause gambling problems for some. At the same time, Christian People's Party, like the government, is concerned that bingo can be maintained as a concept. Traditional paper bingo does not have the same addiction problem, but can, on the contrary, be good social meeting places. The Christian People's Party therefore supports the objective of the amendment, namely to strengthen traditional bingo operations at the expense of the more aggressive electronic offerings. Today's terminals are to be replaced by terminals from Norwegian Typing. These are machines that are easier to control and that are less aggressive. At the same time, these vending machines will provide a certain income base for both bingo contractors and local teams and associations. There is no doubt that this case is also about compensating the bingo contractors to some extent. But the core of this case is to ensure control over a part of the gaming market that has not been regulated well enough so far, and it supports the Christian People's Party. Then there have been some reports of concern from voluntary teams and associations related to percentages and distribution principles, which have already been discussed by many. Some are concerned that not all income from Norwegian Typing machines should be included in the income base for the key key distribution. But there are also many who have a different vision. The most important thing for the Christian People's Party is that we take care of two important considerations: incomes for volunteering, teams and associations, and that we limit gambling dependence. We believe that the measures that are now being taken take account of these considerations, and we therefore support the proposals. In the case of the 40 pst key. to Norwegian Typing, 35 pst. for bingo contractors and 25 pst. It is important to closely monitor developments in teams and associations. Here we need to consider how this turns out after a certain period of time, and the majority of the committee also specifies that the key should be adjusted by means of a separate regulation in the years following the introduction of this amendment.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2453	Last June, Stortinget dealt with the Riksrevision's report on the extended revision of Norwegian Typing. The majority, all except the Progress Party, said on the Control and Constitutional Committee that they asked the Ministry to ‘assess whether the accounting audit of Norsk Tipping can be carried out in the future by external auditors’ instead of by the National Audit Office, as the scheme is today. Norwegian Typing is the last public limited liability company that has the Riksaudition as accounting auditor, and I ask the Progress Party to note that they believe this is a breach of current practice. This is to carry out audits in line with what we do in other areas, including: by NRK. The auditor may then be selected as a private audit firm with continuous experience of auditing of a number of types of limited liability companies. In my opinion, it provides better assurance that this company will be properly audited. But the Riksrevision, like the Swedish National Audit Authority, will continue to play an important role in relation to Norwegian Typing AS. The fact that the Riksaudition will now receive an audit report from an external auditor for its audit work will, in my opinion, strengthen this work. Such an assessment has also been based on the revision of the Bank of Norway and other independent state-owned institutions. I am pleased that the majority of the committee has joined the proposal for a change in the law on gambling. It has been a good tradition for the last ten years to say that the Storting has largely been behind the main lines of gambling policy. I also think it is important that this tradition continues. It provides the best assurance that we can prevent social problems related to gambling. At the same time, the Norwegian regulation with exclusive rights to single players for the largest gambling companies provides efficient operations and a limited scope for private profits from the gambling market. It thus ensures maximum profits for sport, culture and non-governmental organisations. The turnover in the bingo halls has increased from 1.9 billion. DKR in 2006 until approx. 5.5 B.C.E. kr in 2010. The sharp increase is due to the increased turnover of fast electronic games in the bingo halls. It has also contributed to increased revenue for the teams and associations receiving profits from bingo operations. The increase coincides entirely with the ban on vending machines which entered into force on 1 July 2007. Increased turnover to bingo halls is positive, but there is a lot of evidence that bingo rooms have taken over part of the problematic gambling activity that we saw earlier on the vending machines. The Ministry therefore decided to ban bingo vending machines from 1 April 2010 and has adopted restrictions on the rules on fast computer games. Norwegian Typing is about to place new games in bingo halls. This will also be fast games, but these games will be subject to more stringent restrictions and controls. We know that it's the fast games that can create addiction, and that's why we need to have better control over these. So Norwegian Typing has taken over the responsibility for the fastest gambling in the bingo halls, and we get stronger state control of these. Without the measures we have now adopted, we will be able to have growing problems with gambling addiction in bingo rooms. We have also recorded this by game phone for those who are game addicts. Many of those who previously depended on vending machines may seem to have moved to other types of games, and they have increasingly contacted the auxiliaries. In the light of the examination by the Storting of the proposal for the allocation of profits from bingo terminals, the Ministry will adopt a regulation regulating the distribution of profits from these terminals. I'm adding that the distribution is going to be 40 pst. to Norwegian Typing to cover investment and operating costs, 35 pst. for bingo contractors and 25 pst. to the teams and associations that receive profits from bingo operations. This distribution has been discussed with the actors involved in the handling of the bill by Storting. The general rule that the Norwegian Typing game's surplus is to be distributed through the key is to be determined. However, in a transitional phase, the profits of Norwegian Typing terminals in bingo halls should be substantially returned to the bingo facilities. Bingo revenues are likely to decline in the coming years, because bingo machines were banned in 2010 and because the rules for computer bingo become more stringent from 2012. With transitional arrangements, we can prevent bingo spaces from falling away, and the decline in incomes to teams and associations is too great. If it would be appropriate to change the distribution in the long term, regulatory changes will be made after ordinary consultation.

In discussions with ESA, on the other hand, the practice of the law, that is, the practice that has given us the ringing effects we see today, has not been contrary to the EEA Agreement. That is why, with the agreement of ESA, the Ministry of Oil and Energy has drawn up a proposal for a new legal text which respects current practice and which is in line with our European commitments. What matters to the red-green government – and to the vast majority – is that it is confirmed. The new wording will preserve current practice better than the previous wording did. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2601	The background to the legislative change we are handling is, as mentioned earlier, that the EFTA Surveillance Authority, ESA, has assessed the petroleum law in relation to Norway’s EEA obligations and found that the Norwegian legal text does not fully comply with the EEA Agreement. On the other hand, the proposal refers to the fact that the Norwegian practice of the legal text, i.e. the Norwegian administration attached to the petroleum industry, does not conflict with the EEA Agreement. Following dialogue with ESA, the government has put forward proposals for changes to the legal text that, according to the Ministry, make it consistent with current practice and with the obligations we have under the EEA Agreement. In the proposition, the government says that the legal entities must ensure that they are represented so that they can make informed decisions about the petroleum industry in Norway at any time, and that it will depend on the size, scale and complexity of the activity of the individual legal entity, including whether it is an operator or not. This, together with the assurance given by the government that this will not change what is Norwegian practice today, is an important premise for the Central Party in this matter. The Committee has organised a hearing and received views from a number of stakeholders. Among others, Professor of European Law at the University of Oslo, Finn Arnesen, points out that the legal wording of the current law was so open in breach of the EEA Agreement that it should be clear to the politicians at the time of the conclusion of the agreement that the law would not be enforceable without conflicting with the obligations of the EEA Agreement. Nor from any other point of view did anything come up that would allow you to stick to today's legal text without conflicting with the EEA Agreement. The centre party is against the EEA Agreement, and our alternative is to the European Union through a free trade agreement. There's something we've been raising up before, so that's everybody in this room who knows about it. We pointed out at the time of the conclusion of the EEA Agreement, precisely to the extent of the agreement, and to the dynamics of the EEA Agreement, which we see – over and over again – as the agreement becomes more and more extensive. The policy objectives for the petroleum industry to have a ring-fenced effect in the form of economic activity and employment are established. There is no doubt that the EEA Agreement limits Norwegian powers of government in this area as well. We assume that the decision we are making today does not change the practice that we have incorporated into Norwegian administration. There are many reasons why a decentralised base and operating structure and the representation of the companies in Norway are being maintained, not least in terms of health, environment and safety that one should be close to these activities. Nevertheless, I would remind you that regional policy concerns, or other considerations based on policy and ideology, do not have the same authority in this area – as in many other areas after the EEA Agreement was introduced.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2668	The protection of the economic interests of their own countries — and in the field of industrial policy — has been a long way off in the European context, and since, among other things, Ireland and Iceland met the wall as deregulated paradises, it will continue to protect their own interests by many stamped as petty nationalism or the process of thinking of the Septuagint that stands in the way of a free and open Europe. It does not change the practice. It is underlined in the new legal text that the government can impose specific requirements on the rights holder’s organisation in Norway. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2685	This is a matter that had deserved far more attention than it has received. Rarely has there been a more vigorous attempt by a government to reduce, obscure and conceal a politically sensitive matter than what we have witnessed in this matter. On 17 September 2008, the government launched a dialogue with ESA on the wording of Section 10-2 of the Petroleum Act. The disagreement arose as to whether Norway has the opportunity to impose requirements on the organisation and use of bases by rightholders in Norway. The current legal text states that rightholders shall have an organisation capable of directing the petroleum activity independently from Norway and that the petroleum activity shall be operated from base in Norway. The dialogue with ESA took place for one and a half years without results and ended with an opening letter from ESA in January 2010 which concluded that the Act on Petroleum § 10-2 is a breach of Article 31 and Article 36 of the EEA Agreement. Since January 2010, the government has been quietly negotiating a solution with ESA that eliminates virtually all requirements for the organisation and use of bases by rightholders in Norway. The government has not bothered to inform the Storting about ESA’s opening letter, as it has established practice since the 1990s. After the negotiations were concluded, the government has failed to send the case to the hearing as required by the Administration Act and the investigation manual. Why? In a letter to Stortinget, the Council of State explained that the reason why Stortinget was not informed about the letter of opening was that the government had the intention to keep the legal text of the day. The explanation that the case was not sent to the hearing afterwards is that one had agreed with ESA on a wording which it was not possible to change through a consultation round. The ministry's correspondence with the civil ombudsman, who went on at the same time as the case was being dealt with here in Stortinget, shows another reality. The Minister’s letter of 11 May 2011 gives the following reasons for refusal of transparency: ‘Surveillance of that ESA letter could lead to a press review of the case or a publicity from others, and this was politically important for the government to prevent. This was not at all important for the groups on the Stortinget, which would easily get the wrong impression of the case if it was misinterpreted publicly or uncritically before agreement was reached between the Ministry and ESA on a solution.» The Christian People's Party reacted to this matter in good time before there was a matter in the media. Our proposal then was that the matter should be sent to the general hearing, and this was submitted as a note to the committee one week before the evening post published the department's correspondence with the civil ombudsman. At that time we had a suspicion that the matter was more important than it was produced by the government, which we have clearly marked in retrospect. If it is really the case that the proposed legislative changes are merely an adaptation to current practice and that they are so insignificant that the Storting and the public did not need to be involved in the process, why has the government negotiated with ESA for two and a half years on the matter? Why was it then the government's intention to keep today's wording after the opening letter from ESA was received in January 2010, one and a half years after the dialogue with ESA started? And then why did the department as they did to the civil ombudsman, if this wasn't so important? The matter speaks for itself. I have rarely experienced similar embarrassing explanation in a matter that has been promoted for treatment in Stortinget. The Christian People's Party takes this seriously. The Christian People's Party believes that the proposed changes may result in significantly less local ring-effects of future increased petroleum activity in North Norway. Norway is now facing a new era of oil and gas extraction. The centre of gravity moves northwards. We're going to have oil and gas extraction in North Norway. The people of the north have high expectations of the activities and jobs on land. The government's broad proposals may put an end to this. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e2885	Over 17 years of the EEA Agreement, the Norwegian political authorities have maintained that Section 10-2 of the Petroleum Act and its management and base requirements can be maintained and are in line with our international obligations under the EEA Agreement. This is a strong and important position. The Petroleum Act Section 10-2 sets out requirements for management and bases. The management requirement may be waived on a case-by-case basis, which has been the content of the paragraph since the derogation was introduced in 2003. Today's paragraph ensures that management is still required, while allowing for the opening of exemptions. Derogations from the management requirement are therefore in line with the paragraph and do not involve a practice which requires the amendment of the paragraph. If the ESA or the companies consider that Norwegian law and its practice are not in line with our obligations under the EEA Agreement, they may bring the matter before the EFTA Court. This has still not happened – more than 17 years after the entry into force of the EEA Agreement, and 16 years after the concession directive was implemented in Norwegian law. In other words, the burden of proof after the current section 10-2 of the Petroleum Act lies on ESA and on the companies. That should still be the case. Through the historical fact, the many opinions expressed by EEA members on the position of the Petroleum Act in the EEA Agreement and the examination of this case, it is not established that the new wording of Section 10-2 presented is the only possible wording that could fulfil our obligations under the EEA Agreement. This speaks for the Norwegian authorities to allow more time in this case and, among other things, to do so. it sees in conjunction with the notified petroleum message. Such a process will also allow the Storting to go further into whether current practice is in fact in line with the new paragraph, especially in cases where Norwegian authorities and companies have divergent views on management and bases. On the basis of this, it would also be wise to resume negotiations with ESA on requirements for the organisation and management of rightholders.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3037	We are now going to deal with the issue of partial toll financing for Fosenpaket. Phosphorus packages are projects and measures that are part of the development of today's county road network on Fosenhalv Island in the South and North Trøndelag. Phosphorus packages are a priority in the county action programme. Phosphorus packages include 17 projects divided into three subpackages, with projects in order of priority. All projects are located in Fosenhalvøya, with the exception of the sub-project fv. 710 Ingdal–Valset, located in the municipality of Agdenes. Four projects are wholly or partly located in the Nord-Trøndelag. The rest is in the South-Trøndelag. The proposal for toll arrangements, to which it is a local policy commitment, provides for the financing of projects and actions within Sub-packages 1 and 2. The main road network on Fosen has a low standard. The roads are narrow and curved with low visibility and pass through local residential areas. Phosphorus packages will improve accessibility and road safety. The link between Fosen and Trondheim and internal communication on Fosen will be improved, which can facilitate positive regional development. The total estimated cost of projects and actions in Fosenpaket is about 1.7 billion. kr. The total estimated cost of subpackages 1 and 2 is approximately 1.3 billion. 2011-kronor. There is still uncertainty about the costs of several projects and actions, but there is flexibility in the toll package by allowing the scope of deployment to be assessed in-progress. If any of the projects are more expensive than expected, the lowest priority projects will be taken out of the package or the scope of the individual projects may be reduced. The funding of Fosenpaket is based on county funds and tolls. 75 pst. investment costs are to be financed by tolls. Toll collection is planned in the ferry connections Brekstad-Valset and Flek–Rørvik. In addition, a toll station will be established at fv. 715 at Krinsvatn. It has been suggested that the toll collection should start as soon as possible after the Storting has taken its decision today. It's been added up to two-way collection for 15 years. Traffickers who travel both internally on Fosen and between Fosen and Trondheim benefit from the expansion and are required to pay tolls. The Progress Party promotes its own proposal, which, not surprisingly, opposes toll financing, and proposes a different financing arrangement. ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3336	In 1999, the Storting agreed to partially toll-financed construction of E6 and E18 in Østfold, including measures on existing E18 and cross-roads along E18. The extension has been submitted to the Stortinget in several rounds. The E18 between the Riksgrenst–Örje and Oslo is the main foreign orientator of the country after the E6 between the Riksgrenst/Svinesund and Oslo. About 20 pst. of all international carriage of goods by road to and from Norway carried on E18 through Eastfold. This is a major stretch of heavy transport, and it is the main route to and from Stockholm and the Baltic region. On the approximately 6 km distance east from Momark to Härland, continuous pass-by is planned on both sides of the road. At Härland, a 150 m long wooden-field tunnel shall be built. The remainder of the route up to Middle Town shall be built as a two-field road without pass-by field. Mid-driving works shall be built on the whole route. In recent years, E18 in Eastfold has had an annual traffic growth of 2.5 pst. higher than the national average. In this case, the government is proposing the development and financing of E18 on the Melleby-Momark route, including the financing of actions on existing E18 and cross-roads along the E18 route. Construction start is planned early in 2012 and road opening is summer 2014. The Committee shall be required to do so. In addition, the government outlines an overall approach to how the Parselles Riksgren-Ørje, Melleby-Momarken and Knapstad-Akershus border can be expanded and financed, including the remaining funding of the cross-road measures. There is local political support for the proposed arrangement. The E6 through Østfold was completed as a four-field road in 2008. The E6 toll collection started in 2001. In 2006, it was decided to extend the recovery period from 15 to 20 years for both E6 and E18 in the context of the review of the deployment and financing arrangements for the project E18 Krosby-Knapstad. Further development of E18 through Eastfold was addressed in the context of the national transport plan 2002–2011 and the national transport plan 2006–2015. The size has later joined different arrangements for the maintenance of individual projects. The Eastfold Package has helped significantly improve the road standard of the 58 km route on the E18 through Eastfold in recent years. The E18 is now built as a two-field road between Ørje and Melleby, and as a four-field road from Momarken to Knapstad. Consequently, three subdivisions remain: Riksgren–Ørje 6.6 km Melleby–Momarken, which we treat here today, 8.3 km Knapstad–Akershus border 6.0 km No level clearance is available for the route Riksgren–Örje. There is considerable uncertainty about the cost of developing this route. For the route Melleby-Momarken, there is a approved regulatory plan. An external quality assurance has been carried out. For the Knapstad-Akershus border section, there is a approved municipal sub-plan. The work on the regulatory plan is ongoing. The total estimated cost is approximately 2.3 billion. NOK for the development of E18 on the sections that I have mentioned here, including the remaining financing of cross-road measures. The annual traffic on the route today varies between 7000 and 8000 vehicles. In the dimensioning year, annual traffic of about 10 500 vehicles is expected. In the new toll stations, up to 15 years have been collected in each station. This involves toll collection between 2001 and 2030, a total of 29 years. A total toll contribution amounts to approximately 7.4 billion. kr for E6 and E18. The Government underlines that the financing plan for the E18 Riksgren–Örje does not imply that the government has taken a decision on the extension of the route. The Government will come back with an assessment of this in the context of the roll-out of the national transport plan. It is positive that there is local political support for the projects. There is strong local involvement in Eastfold for a forced deployment of E18 through the county. The various decisions taken in recent years, both locally in the municipalities of Askim, Eidsberg, Hobøl, Spydeberg, Trøgstad and Marker, in the district of Eastfold and in the centre of Stortinget, express the desire to expand. The government wants to avoid stopping in the construction of E18. The majority emphasise that this is not the case in the media, that the Melleby–Momark route ensures continuous development. Since the last trasé opening, the section Krosby–Knapstad in November 2010, it has been stationary in the construction work on the route. If the regulatory plan is approved in the autumn and there are no unfulfilled conditions in the further work, construction starts may be possible as early as 2013. If we want to achieve such a goal, in other words, construction start Knapstad – Akershu’s boundary will be able to take place before Melleby – Momark is finished. Such progress will provide good continuity and coherent expansion. The Progress Party, Right and Christian Folkepari will build the four-field road on the route Melleby-Momarken. The proposition makes it possible for traffic in the year of dimensioning, which is 20 years after the planned opening of the road, to be estimated to be around 8 000 vehicles per day. This is much lower than the 12000 entry point in annual traffic for four fields according to road standards. Four fields in the Melleby–Momark will involve a monthly overcapacity. The consequences would be higher costs and thus less money to go to other places, including the finalisation of the E18 in Eastfold.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3563	Over several years, the focus has now been on the further development of E18 through Eastfold. E18 Ørje – Oslo is a high-traffic foreign ORridor. About 20 pst. of all international carriage of goods by road to and from Norway carried on E18 through Eastfold. It is tempting to add that the rest, about two thirds of the land-based transport of goods and persons to and from Norway, travel on E6 through Eastfold and over Svinesund. This gives us a great deal of traffic congestion through the county and is one of the reasons why we in Eastfold, with our rapporteurs and county rapporteurs at the head, prioritise the development of the main transport years E6, which were completed in 2008, and E18, which we are holding, and also the double-track railway further from Moss and to the border in order not to have to start and extend the E6 to the six-field motorway, as the Progression Party will, before the four-field road is paid down, because of the traffic load on the road. All these projects are part of the original Eastfold package, which was first adopted by the Stortinget in 2000. The development of E18 has been a priority in NTP 2002–2011 and 2006–2015. But it was only during the Stoltenberg II administration that we got speed on the expansion. In 2007, the development and financing of the Krosby-Knapstad section was discussed and adopted in the Stortinget. This route has now been completed and opened by the Prime Minister and the Minister of Transport. Today, we are discussing proposals for the development and financing of the next route, Melleby-Momarken. The municipalities concerned and Østfold County Council examined the case in March and April 2010 and have joined an arrangement for the development and financing of E18 on the remaining sections of the Riksgren–Örje, Melleby–Momarken and Knapstad–Akershus border, including the remaining financing of cross-road measures. But it's only Melleby–Momark that the case applies today. The remaining sections will be returned by the government in connection with the national transport plan in 2013. The development of E18 and E6 is, through the Easternfold package, an overall project, based on the sharing of the costs between the drivers and the state, where the E6 drivers have contributed almost 70 pst. When both roads are completed, there will be a total of 50–50 distribution between the country and the drivers for these two roads, which will be paid down in 2030. With this government, the E18 has been developed continuously and without any stop. It gives predictability to the project and to the inhabitants of Inner Eastfold – and to road users – who are very positive. So we hope that the last sections will be further expanded in the same spirit in the future. According to the Department, annual traffic on the route is between 7 000 and 8 000 vehicles today, and in the year of dimensioning – i.e. 20 years after the planned traffic opening – it is an ODT of about 10 500 vehicles. Based on these figures, the E18 on the Momarken-Melleby route is planned with partly continuous pass-by field, partly with a 150 m long wooden field tunnel, and partly by two-field road without pass-by field. Mid-driving works shall be built on the whole route. I assume that these traffic figures are going to last, and that they're going to build a road for the future. After a spring in which some in Eastfold were nervous that there would not be a continuation of the E18 this year, we now see that the government and the Stortinge return.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3770	The case concerns a proposal from representatives Bård Hoksrud, Jan-Henrik Fredriksen, Inbjørg Godskesen and Arne Sortevik to restrict the use of salt on Norwegian roads. There was an open hearing on the matter. There, Norway's Automobil-Verbund, Stop Veisaltingen and the Norwegian Nature Conservation Association participated. From the participants, a number of disadvantages were identified related to the extensive salt use on Norwegian roads. I refer to the proposal of the Advance Party in the vote. The other parties themselves will explain their views. The Progress Party wants to reduce salt use on Norwegian roads. The proposals of the Progress Party have provided a number of leads to the widest possible assessment related to salt use. The progress batch will have assessed both road safety and environmental aspects. In concrete terms, the Progress Party will have a halve of salt use from the winter season 2012/2013. Subsidy, the Progress Party will support the proposal of the Right and Christian People's Party. We interpret this proposal as an accession of the Right and Christian People's Party to the main wish of the Progress Party: to stop the rapidly increasing salt use and turn the trend towards much less use of salt on Norwegian roads. We are also pleased that the government parties do not reject the proposal, but attach it to the protocol. In this case, we can therefore hope that the ears of the government parties are not completely closed – or sealed with salt. Of course, it may be because the State Road Service has been doing its own salt project for almost four years, Salt SMART, but at least the road surface itself looks at salt use. En-route results have not been visible, on the contrary, the use of salt on Norwegian roads has increased during the 2007-2011 project period. This is not a hot political matter, but a matter that has a great public involvement in road accidents, salt damage on the road and vehicles and salt damage on nature and on water sources – much more than just salt spills on the smooth road. The Progress Party is familiar with the Salt SMART project. We think it's good that the building itself looks at this. But we want a broader perspective – we want input from competence outside the road network itself to ensure that ideas, hypotheses, calculations and strategies on salt use in winter maintenance of Norwegian roads are challenged. That is what the Progress Party believes is necessary. The Progress Party also notes that the use of salt on Norwegian roads has increased sharply in the four years that the project has been in progress. There are several reasons why the Progress Party addresses the strongly increasing use of salt on Norwegian roads. Salting is a consequence of the bar road strategy, which means that instead of physical bridging, chemical bridging is used. The Progress Party considers the increase and the extent of the use to be a cause for concern. We believe it is necessary to think smart and act smart, but without salt. We see a completely different development in our neighbouring countries Sweden and Finland. There are two countries with which it is natural to compare, which have reduced salt use too far, and which appear to have succeeded much better than Norway in reducing fatalities and serious road accidents. The Party considers that the effect of road salinisation on road safety is far from as clear and documented as it is claimed. The Party considers that this should be verified. The point of progress shows that there are actually good reasons to claim that road salting can provide smoother road conditions and smoother tyres with reduced braking power. The Party of Advances considers that the consequential road damage after road salting is significant, both on the road surface and on the road foundation, and in particular on bridges and in tunnels where the salt dries on concrete and reinforcement iron. In fact, the last to point to consequential damage to concrete was the Landsförbund of the Houseowners in their last magazine, which came recently. In other countries, this is much clearer with consequential salt damage. In the United States, it is established that the consequential damage is 15 times the cost of salting. The party noted that the head of the Salt SMART project believes that salt use can be halved with sensible salt use. This indicates that today's use is unreasonably, at least unwise. This suggests the possibility of a large reduction in salt use. It substantiates, in the view of the Progress Party, the demand of the Progress Party that a significant reduction be possible and that it should be speeded up. It substantiates the claim that practices that have evolved over many years of high and increasing salt use have been unwise.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3804	The representative of Arne Sortevik has addressed the proposal to which he referred.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3807	The discussion on road salinisation is a recurring discussion. It comes every winter, and we record that there are very different perceptions of whether salting is right, both in terms of road safety and the environment. What gives even greater ammunition to this discussion now is the enormous increase we've seen in salt use in recent years. It makes us have to stop and ask questions about whether it's right and important to use salt on the roads. For the Workers' Party, there are two questions that need to be answered: Does salting improve road safety? How does this affect the environment? The progress party already has the answers, we have registered. We're still asking the questions. The state road system, which is our professional body in this context, has so far considered that salting provides better road safety and does not unduly affect the environment. According to the letter from the Minister of Transport to the Committee, the use of salt today is the only real option for achieving good performance and road safety on the most trafficked roads in winter, but it is important to organise the use of salt to limit damage to the environment. According to the Ministry, there are no good alternatives to salt to maintain good friction throughout the winter on high-traffic roads. We are also aware that this year the State Road Administration will complete the 4-year R & D programme Salt SMART. The aim of this programme is to find out how to reduce the use of salt while maintaining good visibility and road safety during winter. The project must already have resulted in increased competence on the effects and consequences of the use of salt. As a result, adjustments are made each year to the operating contracts and their follow-up. According to the Ministry of Transport, the final results of Salt SMART will form the basis for the State Road Administration’s proposal for a strategy for the winter operation of Norwegian roads in the proposal for the National Transport Plan 2014-2023, and the government will present this to the Stortinget in the NTP winter 2013. With regard to the effect on road safety when using salt, there are also shared opinions, but the Traffic Safety Manual indicates an average effect of 15 pst. reduction in the number of injuries caused by the use of salt. We are pleased that during the course of the year the State Road Service will carry out an independent and up-to-date review of recent relevant research on the effect of salt on road safety, and look forward to its results. There is a professional disagreement about the use of road salt and the impact on road safety and the environment. We had a hearing on the representative proposal where this became very clear. The working party feels that it is urgent to put in place a new strategy for winter maintenance based on new knowledge and research on impacts both on road safety and the environment, and we therefore call for this work to be carried out. We do not have to wait until the roll-out of the national transport plan. It should be possible to speed it up. We are also concerned that the Committee's consultation on the proposal for a representative made claims that contractors salt more than what is contained in the contracts, because this is cheaper than mechanical removal of snow and ice. Therefore, we assume that the State Road Service has control procedures that detect and correct such conditions. It is totally unsustainable to use salt instead of mechanical removal of snow and ice just to save money, whether this is part of, or not part of, the operating contracts. Summary: There is a technical disagreement as to whether the use of road salt is positive for road safety and the environment. We believe it is urgent to clarify this, and ask for the work to be carried out. We also believe that it is totally unacceptable if it means that contractors salt more than what is in the contracts because this is cheaper than mechanical removal of snow and ice. This must be corrected as soon as possible.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3921	We're salting a lot on our roads. Salt consumption will vary from year to year, based on climate variations and the winter strategy for operations implemented. Salt use has increased since 2006. It's a challenge that both I and the State Road Administration are taking seriously. A lot of work is done to apply less salt to future maintenance. Salt may be harmful to fresh water, soil and vegetation along the road network. A study a few years ago showed that many lakes along the most trafficked road network have standing, low-oxygen bottom water as a result of road salting. The state road system is now re-examined in fresh water along the road network. For water sources, the requirement for drinking water is important. The state road authority cooperates with the Food Authority and local waterworks to ensure that vegetal salt does not contaminate drinking water. As far as the state road system is concerned, there is no waterworks in the vicinity of being above the maximum level that is legal for the content of chloride and sodium in drinking water due to road salting. Despite the fact that the salting has unfortunate environmental effects that we have to counteract, it is still such that salting is an important tool in winter maintenance. The need for road safety is important here. Roads must be clear of snow and ice to such an extent that wintering is safe. Even if we want to reduce salt use, especially in terms of the environment, it's not going to come from the fact that the use of salt under the right conditions gives better grip and removes snow and ice efficiently. Here I underline the right properties; salt is not a universal resource. It has to be, for example: not used when cold. The efforts to reduce the use of salt take place through several processes. There are many environments that research these questions, both in Norway and internationally. Let me particularly point to the State Road Administration's R & D project Salt SMART. The aim of the project is that the State Road Agency's efforts to maintain the emergence and traffic safety in winter should not cause unacceptable damage to the environment. Salt SMART focuses on the distance salt consumption, with a special emphasis on reducing the consumption of sections that are particularly vulnerable to natural damage. The work on Salt SMART has been concentrated on improving knowledge by identifying problem areas, developing methodology, documenting operational phase experience, etc. One must also gain experience from other countries with the same climate as ours, where one has managed to reduce salt use. The state road authority states that the results of Salt SMART will be timely so that we can use the results of work on the National Transport Plan 2014-2023. Although this is a priority work, we will unfortunately not be able to change salt practices overnight. The reason for this is that new requirements have to be tested. These requirements will be gradually incorporated into external environmental plans, in manuals and in the competitive basis for new supply. An important grip now is, among other things, to identify new mechanical methods for the effective removal of snow and ice from roads. The Road Service of the State, as I have written to the Committee, will carry out during the year an independent and up-to-date review of newer and relevant exploration of its impact on road safety. It's an important job. The state's road system prioritises both getting its salt effect on cleanness and at the same time developing new ways of effectively removing snow and ice from the roads. I believe that what has emerged through the Committee's hearing underlines the importance of this work.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e3992	This proposal is important. Even if we don't get an impact on that today, I'm pretty sure we're going to get right in our claims. Despite the Salt SMART project, to which the others refer, today we are cleaning out the road salt that we have never done before. 330000 tonnes are pulverised on the roads, with no evidence of good credibility in terms of road safety gains. Sweden and Finland reduce their use of salt. Sweden and Finland have no worse traffic damage than Norway — on the contrary. They have far fewer serious accidents than us, despite the fact that they use far less salt than us. In the United States and Canada, road salt is categorised as poison, and according to their research, the effect in a maintenance perspective is that road salting results in up to 15 times more expensive operation and maintenance of roads. On a journey The party's fraction did in Poland, where we studied motorways and how they dealt with salting, we saw that it was constructed viaducts after the motorways – miles by mile – that collected the road salt and led it to large collection chambers. And why? Of course, the Poles are aware that this is a poison, and they don't want to spread this poison into nature. Has anyone, ever, thought at all in Norway? Here we put out a poison. The use of salt has exploded during the Salt SMART project. Even our own drinking water reservoirs are being added to this poison. Salting as much as is being done today can best be compared to peeing in your pants. It warms for a little while, but physically, how boring it gets after some time. Yes, the Vegvessen saves money there and there, but the bill over time I'm ugly that can be incredibly much higher than most people are able to dream about. We want to spend more money on winter maintenance without salt – no doubt about it – but society as a whole will make enormous use of less salt. We know that the greatest impacts of ice explosion occur in the transition from ice to water. Normally, our pathways will experience this process a few times a year without salt, but with salt this chemical process that the salt produces will lead to the ice explosion going on almost continuously for months. It's not that our roads are without cracks, cracks, and holes that make the salt not need to penetrate almost everywhere, not least into the very foundation of the road? What effect this has on roads, maintenance and damage is hanging in the air, but I can't possibly imagine that it's positive.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e4397	Norway’s membership in NATO and Norwegian NATO policies is widely welcomed, as confirmed by these settings. Through this, we ensure Norway’s security and at the same time contribute to stability and cooperation in our part of the world. However, a prerequisite for good military cooperation is that we also succeed on a political level. With developments in which the EU countries in the alliance go a long way in coordinating defence and security policy through EU bodies, it is particularly important to ensure the political part of NATO cooperation. We are not served by developments in which NATO remains the main military actor, while the main political discussions on European and global security take place in the EU. As is well known, Norway’s main input to the NATO debate on a new strategic concept was the so-called neighbourhood initiative. I fully share the government's assessment of the importance of clarifying the balance between NATO's core tasks and operations outside NATO's geographical impact field. NATO needs to focus more on the core tasks of the alliance and the challenges of NATO's geographical periphery. Operations far away must not go beyond the collective defence of the territories of NATO countries. We also wholeheartedly support the work to raise issues of disarmament on the agenda. There is every reason to welcome NATO's commitment to work for a world without nuclear weapons in its new concept for the first time. As regards the third priority area of government, it is easy to endorse the importance of close and constructive cooperation with Russia. A lasting basis for peace and stability in our part of the world must include Russia. NATO cooperation is based on consensus. This has led to the establishment of so-called coalitions of willingness in some cases where countries have not agreed to engage in military action. There are situations where this will be necessary, but we should be aware that such coalitions could weaken the political alliance itself, which is glued to cooperation, whether it is outside the formal framework and decision-making bodies of NATO cooperation. The communication on cooperation in NATO in 2010 shows great activity on many fronts and with the ISAF operations in Afghanistan as the main operational challenge. The decision to initiate the transfer of responsibility for security to the Afghan authorities in 2011 marks the start of a new phase of the alliance’s efforts in the country. At the request of the United Nations, NATO is contributing positively to the fight against piracy outside the Horn of Africa through the Ocean Shield operation. NATO’s involvement in Kosovo shall be progressively reduced as local authorities become able to ensure the security of the area. Work to implement UN Resolution 1325 on women, peace and security in NATO continues. It is gratifying that the government has developed its own action plan to address the needs and rights of women in conflict areas. It is an important area that must not be forgotten in the name of the fight. Technological developments make our modern societies vulnerable to attacks on technical installations. Therefore, protection against cyberattack or so-called cyberdefence becomes an increasingly important field of action. Knowledge and ability to address threats both to military installations and basic civil devices will be essential. The NATO summit in Lisbon therefore devotes considerable attention to this issue. Up-to-date data protection policy is being addressed during 2011. NATO's partnership policy is an important part of the alliance's activities. It is based on two pillars: a practical part in which NATO offers various forms of assistance and training for defence reform, increased security for civil society and cooperation with NATO forces, and a more political part dealing with political dialogue and support for reform processes. In the year that passed, this activity included, inter alia: Russia, Balkan countries, Mediterranean countries, Ukraine and Georgia. Work on reforming the NATO organisation is continuing. An important objective is to establish a new command structure with fewer headquarters and sub-organisations and a reduction in the number of posts, from 13 000 to 9 000. An effective committee structure and good internal working processes are a necessary part of this conversion. Like the rest of the world, NATO member states have also been hit hard by the financial crisis, leading to pressure on national defence budgets and requiring closer cooperation between allies in the development and purchase of military capabilities. Let me then, for the sake of the many people who had to read the minutes from the debate and miss a few words about the operation in Libya, just stress that the reason it is not mentioned in the Innst. 413 S or in my post, this setting concerns the 2010 report, i.e. before the Libya operation was started.

If we don't have participants in a body that meets to meet, and it's really a bad way to manage taxpayers' money, and not least the time for those who are there doing their job down there. All in all, my conclusion is that NATO is a cornerstone of our foreign and security policy. The organisation plays on many facets – from the purest military operation, the sharpest piece of what NATO does, to the civil piece. Here is a wide range of measures and a wide variety of measures – not least the commitment that nations contribute to, and perhaps most importantly: the efforts of each individual who is out doing a job, which they actually set up and take responsibility for.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e4446	The rapporteur has given a thorough account of the Committee's position. Of course, we agree with the wording that the rapporteur has made today. There is little doubt that NATO today appears to be our most sovereign foreign policy arena. There we are, in fact, at parliamentarian level 'hands on' and are involved in political decision-making. I think that the development of NATO's new strategic concept in November 2010 has illustrated in many ways. There is no doubt that Norway has set its feet on both the government level and the parliamentary level. This institution of which many have predicted the liquidation now appears to be more potent than ever, and will appear to be the traffic police in the world capable of settling where the germ of unrest and conflict could arise. There is little doubt that NATO is in many ways an international political paradox. The paradox has its reason that NATO is the only military alliance in history that has managed to survive its old enemies. In fact, researchers and other political analysts have to a large extent not fully understood why the alliance has survived, and that it is still perceived as useful and relevant. It is a security policy instrument 20 years after the dissolution of the Soviet Union and the Warsaw Pact. So the fact that NATO survived the Cold War makes NATO a unique phenomenon in international politics. So both the case rapporteur and the representative of Ellingen have been at the heart of NATO's challenge, the debates, this political arena that we are given the opportunity to participate in with the partner country, the Middle East, and not least that Norway has prioritised NATO's involvement in Russia's issue. This, I think, is very important, and my experience -- I think it's most of the experience -- is that the NATO Alliance and Russia have drawn close. There is no doubt that there are common denominators that bind NATO and Russia, and it can in the long run create an even greater and better ground for stable and peaceful development. Keywords like Iran, punctuation like terror, etc. has gradually become keywords that create a common denominator. It is also important to draw attention to the Lisbon Summit, which was the most important of many years. The summit was a success for the alliance, but not least for Secretary General Anders Fogh Rasmussen personally, both because he led the alliance to agreement on the strategic concept, and also because he personally played a central role in the development of the agreement between Russia and NATO on the missile defence system. Thus, after the summit, the Secretary-General appears to be a political leader to much greater extent supported by the alliance member countries than before. Then the representative Ellingsen was inside the US's retiring Secretary of Defense, who has expressed his concern about burden-sharing, for Europe, how to deal with defence and security policy. I think there is every reason to note the concern of the US Secretary of Defense, and we have seen this trend in development even after the period covered by this report, namely the year 2010. The introduction to 2011 was a clear expression to deal with the Middle East conflict, NATO sets up under the auspices of UN Decisions, etc. But Europe's security, our own security and our own policies cannot evolve in a situation where we see that the United States' share of NATO's total expenditure rises dangerously high; it has increased by about a quarter. 25 pst. during the last 10 years. The US share of NATO's operating costs starts gradually to balance at a point between 75 and 80 pst, and this should not develop. At the same time, it will be the challenge of the alliance to ensure that we continue to be involved, is ‘hands on’, and that the alliance as such can contribute to the new major powers, for example. India and China, also now contribute to taking their share of responsibility for peace and development in the world. It no longer holds to say that now other countries have to stand still to clean up Syria. It's been done in Libya, and it's not always that you can afford in the United States to assume any future commitment to peace and development.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e4463	Now this isn't a controversial announcement, but it's good to read that there's an overall committee that's behind it. And that brings me right to a comment on the NATO Parliamentary Assembly: the parliamentary element is very important in NATO, I have experienced it over the last few years. Next week I will be attending the Parliamentary Assembly in Tromsø, and it is an expression that in NATO democracies parliamentarians play a very important role in relation to anchoring and inspiring the decisions we make in the critical areas regulated by NATO. So to the representative of Ellingen, I just want to confirm it: it's an important forum. It is important for us to get Norwegian priorities as well, because it is winning back in the countries affected. I want to say here that last year's NATO Summit was an important milestone in the development of the alliance. I would therefore like to comment on a matter that has not been so much discussed, which concerns the changes and modernisations in NATO's command structure that were decided upon, where the number of officers would be reduced from 13 000 to 8 800. The task of determining where the cuts were to be taken was left to the defence ministers. Such decisions are never simple. First of all, the cuts come in the operating command. The headquarters of Lisbon, Madrid, Heidelberg, Naples and Izmir are located. New and smaller headquarters are established in some of these locations, but overall the goal has been achieved from the summit. It became early clear in this period that no significant changes would be made to the transformation command, in which the Joint Warfare Centre on Jåttå is subject. This has been adopted on Jåttå in the new command structure, and it is an important victory for Norway and also a good compliment to the quality of that command. I want to mention this. So a comment on NATO's position here we are now standing, mid-2011, and it's important things that have come about after this message was written, namely the operation in Libya. I believe that last week, Minister of Defence Gates’ speeches were a liberating reflection – again – on the fact that there are democracies we are talking about. That an outgoing defence minister spends time talking straight out and giving clear messages, we're going to take that with us. We can also take with us that he here acknowledged the efforts that Norway has made in the Libyan operation, without paying too much attention to this. But in order to put his posts in perspective, it's important that the Americans also hear back that the NATO allies are participating too fully and are taking a significant cost and burden in the operation in Afghanistan, which is a challenge for all NATO countries, but which is an operation where the United States has led and on which the Allies have ended. So the burden-sharing principle has to be seen in a slightly larger perspective than the exact percentages, as Gates put it. With regard to Norway's role, I feel that the northern regions are now better understood in NATO. It is not an area of increased tension and need for a ‘turbulence planning’ in the defence alliance, but it is a region that is changing politically, and how the proximity of Russia and cooperation with Russia in the Arctic and in the Barents region is an important dimension – historical – to this contrast that was between NATO and Russia. It is still the case that the border we are now completing with Russia was formerly the East-West border, the NATO-Warszawapakt border. This is now a limit where we can show a lot of new cooperation, and I think that is an important Norwegian contribution to the overall understanding that is in NATO. Last week we were able to mark the 20th anniversary of the independence of the Baltic States, and they bring to the square a completely different experience in that neighbouring relationship than we do. What's special about NATO is that we combine our experience with what becomes a common denominator. I think that's also going to be an important part in the future. Finally, I would like to say that it is through our NATO allies that we are participants in a very demanding military operation in Afghanistan, which is now in its transition to Afghan security responsibilities. And we're engaged in Libya through a NATO command. That underlines the seriousness of the relationship with NATO. NATO is not a historical compliance, but an organisation that is most capable of being relevant and of responding to needs and challenges here and now.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-06-14.ana.ud1999e11800	Two proposals have been put forward during the debate. It's proposal No 1 by Arne Sortevik, on behalf of the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, the Verification of Credentials and Immunities, on proposal No. 2, from Øyvind Halleraker on behalf of the Right and Christian People's Party First of all, there is a vote on proposal No. 1 from the Progress Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government is asked to establish a separate sample with the participation of the departments concerned to present a new strategy on the use of salt on Norwegian roads with a view to a significant reduction in the use of salt with effect from the winter season 2012/2013. The size assumes that: environmental damage to water and nature and damage to the infrastructure/accompaniment costs of salt use are emphasised, the effect of road salinisation on road safety is given a particularly thorough assessment. The size points in particular to the need to consider the introduction of specific winter road traffic measures to ensure road safety through the use of less road salt, as has been done in other countries that have reduced the use of salt on the road, taking into account the environment of competence outside the State Road Service and the Road Directorate and other departments concerned. Work should be completed in the spring of 2012 so that a new salt strategy can take effect in the winter of 2012/2013, according to which the Stortinget is appropriately informed about the salt strategy and the specific salt use objectives and for road safety in the winter.’»
